L1086: Medical Officers' comment-
L1087: The frequency of events is too low to realistically be able to assign a drug related effect. The fact that the blood pressure changes returned to normal on the follow up values for subject 1021 is reassuring. Whereas subject 1706 did not have a follow up HR listed after the final recorded value of 114 the increase in HR occurred several weeks after the study visit and so is not relevant.
L1088: 7.4.4 Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
L1089: ECGs were done at 60 min post dose as that was considered Tmax.
L1090: The mean time to reach maximal observed concentration (Tmax) was 1.2 hours after oral administration in AGHD patients and 0.82 hours in normal controls. The t½ was 3.56 hours in AGHD patients and 2.77 hours in normal controls.
L1091: ECG abnormalities were seen in two subjects:
L1092: 1) Subject 0930 was a 38-year-old black male with a history of posttraumatic stress disorder, essential hypertension and obesity (365 lb) and no history of coronary artery disease, smoking, dyslipemia or diabetes. No family history of cardiac disease was recorded. A normal ECG was recorded two months prior to dosing with macimorelin, whereas non-specific T wave abnormalities were noted on an ECG recorded at screening approximately 1 month prior to dosing with macimorelin. Per protocol, an ECG was recorded pre-dose, which was reported as normal and 60 minutes post-dose which was reported as showing new T wave inversions in the anterior leads. ECGs recorded after dosing showed T wave abnormality as well as prolongation of the Bazzett's corrected QT interval (QTc) from 440 ms to a maximum of 501 ms per ECG computer-generated reading (or 490 ms, per manual reading of an external expert reviewer) occurring 3.5 hours post-dose. The subject was asymptomatic the entire time. Due to the T wave inversions, the subject was taken for cardiac catheterization immediately and found to have normal coronary arteries. He was then admitted to the cardiology service to rule out an acute coronary syndrome and for telemetry observation overnight. Due to this overnight observation in a hospital setting, the event qualified as a serious AE. He had a normal echocardiogram, no evidence of abnormal electrolytes and repeated measurements of cardiac enzymes showed no elevated concentrations. His T waves and QTc interval returned to his baseline (i.e., nonspecific T wave changes and QTc = 448 ms) the following day and he was discharged to home.
L1093: Medical officer's comments-
L1094: A QT IRT consult was obtained in April 2011 to help review this case. They summarized the ECG findings using both Bazett's and Fridericia's formula corrections (see Table 9), and came to the conclusions that although PK samples were not collected at all ECG time points, that up to 2.5 hours post-dose macimorelin plasma concentration remained about constant instead of declining (see Table 10), suggesting that the reported QT prolonging effect in this subject is probably linked to macimorelin exposure.

L1105: This medical reviewer asked the applicant to submit the original ECGs from this patient for review. It was noted in these ECG print outs that the machine generated times were crossed out in 4 of the 5 ECGs from the day of the macimorelin testing, (b) (6). From the cases where the original time was still visible through the scratch marks the adjusted time difference was 14 minutes in one case and 52 minutes in the other case. The original ECG machine time is not visible for the 0 and 60 min time points so these times can't be corroborated. Given that these ECG print outs were done within 2 hours of each other it seems likely that they were done using the same ECG machine so it is strange that the correction for
L1106: the time printed out on the machine with the actual time the test was performed would have been different for the two recorded times. These findings do not represent major protocol violations but are consistent with the sloppy bookkeeping observed during the study site inspections (See section 3.2 Compliance with Good Clinical Practices) and add to the concern over the integrity of the submitted data given that the applicant is still not able to submit data to support the inclusion criteria for at least ten patients.
L1107: The original ECG recorded 2 months prior to the study ( (b) (6) ) was normal and did not show any T wave inversion. The ECG at 1 month prior to dosing ( (b) (6) ) showed some T wave inversions in V2 and V3 and was read by the machine as nonspecific T wave abnormality. The predose -15min ECG ( (b) (6) ) was read as normal by the machine although this medical reviewer can see trace T wave inversions in V2 and V3 (QT/QTc=420/440). Following dosing all of the subsequent ECGs show clear T wave inversions in V2, V3 and V4 and maybe even in V1 and a suggestion of ST elevation in V2 (QT/QTc=442/467 at 60min to 452/477 at 175min). The T wave abnormalities appear to be greatest up to the 205min time point (QT/QTc=452/501) although they are still clearly evident at 9 hrs (QT/QTc=436/493) and 22 hours (QT/QTc=418/466) post dose. The final ECG from the day after dosing (22 hours) still shows T wave inversions in V2, V3 and V4 even though the QT/QTc had returned to baseline according to the applicant. One month later ( (b) (6) ) the patient's QT was totally back to normal at 398, no QTc was reported.
L1108: Figure 8 QTc vs. Plasma Macimorelin Levels for Subject 0930
L1109: Figure 8 is a three-panel line graph titled "Macimorelin (ng/mL) &amp; QTc (ms) vs. Time (hr)". The x-axis for all panels is "Time (hr)" ranging from 0 to 25. The top panel shows "QTc Fridericia's correction" (ms) on the y-axis, ranging from 460 to 500. A blue line represents the smoothed data, showing a peak of approximately 495 ms at 7 hours. The middle panel shows "QTc Bazett's correction" (ms) on the y-axis, ranging from 430 to 500. A red line represents the smoothed data, showing a peak of approximately 500 ms at 7 hours. The bottom panel shows "Macimorelin" (ng/mL) on the y-axis, ranging from -2 to 10. A green line represents the data points, showing a peak of approximately 10 ng/mL at 0.75 hours, a drop to approximately 5 ng/mL at 2.5 hours, and a rebound to approximately 9 ng/mL at 3 hours. The legend indicates: Smooth(QTc Fridericia's correction) in blue, Smooth(QTc Bazett's correction) in red, and Macimorelin in green.Figure 8: Three-panel graph showing QTc and Macimorelin levels over time for Subject 0930. The top panel shows QTc (ms) with Fridericia's correction (blue line), peaking at ~495 ms around 7 hours. The middle panel shows QTc (ms) with Bazett's correction (red line), peaking at ~500 ms around 7 hours. The bottom panel shows Macimorelin (ng/mL) levels, with a peak of ~10 ng/mL at 0.75 hours, a drop to ~5 ng/mL at 2.5 hours, and a rebound to ~9 ng/mL at 3 hours. The x-axis for all panels is Time (hr) from 0 to 25.
L1110: The macimorelin plasma levels show a C_{max} at 0.75 hour with typical half-life decay over the four following samples for the first 2 hours. Then for some reason the drug level increased to almost C_{max} at 2.5 hours. Unfortunately, there are no plasma samples available for the measurement of drug levels after 2.5 hours to help determine if the 2.5 hour time point was simply a mixed up sample. A rebound in plasma levels once they had dropped to half of C_{max} at 2 hours is difficult to explain unless the patient took another dose of drug or there was delayed absorption of the original dose for some unknown reason, both of which seem unlikely.
L1111: Peak QTc levels occur between 5 and 10 hours well after C_{max} at 0.75 hours assuming the 2.5 hour time point is an artifact and does not represent the start of increasing drug levels at later times. According to Dr. Lau on the Clinical Pharmacology team the decrease in serum levels from 10.1ng/mL at 0.75 hours to approximately half 5.25ng/mL at 2 hours corresponds to a half-life of 1.25hr which is consistent with the median half-life observed in healthy controls of 2.02hr (0.86-22.52hr).
L1112: Of note, this patient was also taking citalopram, trazodone and prazosin. Both citalopram and trazodone are CYP3A4 substrates just like
L1113: macimorelin so these drugs could compete with macimorelin and slow its elimination but they would not explain the rebound increase in drug levels seen at the 2.5 hour time point. Both citalopram and trazodone can cause QT prolongation. Coadministration with macimorelin which would compete for CYP3A4 metabolism could increase exposure of these drugs and this might be responsible for the QT prolongation seen in this patient.
L1114: Abnormally prolonged QTc, &gt; 450 in men and &gt; 470 in women, is associated with a three-fold increased risk in sudden death even after adjusting for other risk factors13. Therefore, it is important to identify drugs that are likely to prolong QTc during chronic therapy. Given that the indication the applicant is seeking for macimorelin in this NDA is for diagnostic testing of GHD which requires only a single dose of drug the potential for an effect on QTc is limited. Therefore, it seems reasonable to permit discretionary use as needed with appropriate ECG monitoring in patients with higher baseline QTc levels or already on other drugs that may affect QT. In the meantime, this medical reviewer would recommend a thorough QTc study to clarify whether macimorelin does in fact prolong the QT interval.
L1115: 2) Subject 1719 was noted to have sinus bradycardia and left axis deviation on all ECGs recorded, including those at screening and pre-dose. At the 120-minute recording his heart rate was lower than on previous recordings at 52 beats per minute and fusion complexes were noted.
L1116: Medical officer's comment-
L1117: Although plasma macimorelin levels are not available for this patient it seems likely that a drug related effect would have been seen sooner than 120 minutes given the drug has a Tmax of about 1 hour and a half-life of about 2 hours in healthy controls. Given the patients past medical history of sinus bradycardia and left axis deviation it is hard to conclude that these findings were drug related.
L1118: 7.4.5 Special Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
L1119: None

L1121: 7.4.6 Immunogenicity
L1122: No information regarding immunogenicity was included in this submission.
L1123: 7.5 Other Safety Explorations
L1124: 7.5.1 Dose Dependency for Adverse Events
L1125: Dose dependency was not studied in this clinical program. All patients received the same dose of 0.5mg/kg in the pivotal trial AEZS-130-047, as a diagnostic for AGHD, and in the multiple dose trial H-27727, in cancer cachexia patients.
L1126: 7.5.2 Time Dependency for Adverse Events
L1127: No time dependency information for adverse events was included in the submission.
L1128: 7.5.3 Drug-Demographic Interactions
L1129: No drug-demographic information was included in the submission.
L1130: 7.5.4 Drug-Disease Interactions
L1131: No drug-disease interaction information was included in the submission.
L1132: 7.5.5 Drug-Drug Interactions
L1133: No drug-drug interaction information was included in the submission. Macimorelin is primarily metabolized via CYP3A4 and as such co-administration with a CYP3A4 inducer might reduce macimorelin plasma levels affecting the diagnostic performance of the test.
L1134: 7.6 Additional Safety Evaluations
L1135: 7.6.1 Human Carcinogenicity
L1136: Carcinogenicity studies were not required as it is unlikely that a single dose of macimorelin would pose a carcinogenic risk to human subjects.
L1137: 7.6.2 Human Reproduction and Pregnancy Data
L1138: No human data is available. No definitive reproductive or developmental toxicity studies were conducted.
L1139: 7.6.3 Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
L1140: Chronic treatment with GH is used to treat growth deficiency in pediatrics. However it is unlikely that the single dose of macimorelin would have any long lasting effect on pediatric growth.
L1141: 7.6.4 Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal and Rebound
L1142: No information on drug overdose was included in this submission.
L1143: There is a potential for drug abuse by body builders who are seeking oral agents that could stimulate GH release. However as the product will be packaged for single doses as part of a diagnostic test it seems unlikely that potential drug abusers could get enough of a supply to permit the chronic dosing required to get the pharmacodynamic effects they are seeking.
L1144: No information on drug withdrawal or rebound was included in this submission. However such conditions are unlikely to occur with single dose administration.

L1154: 9.2 Labeling Recommendations
L1155: Given the uncertainty with the reproducibility of the cut point in different study populations, it is recommended that the label include sensitivity and specificity for a range of cut points.
L1156: (b) (4)
L1157: Given that the drug can stimulate GH release by acting on receptors on both the hypothalamus and pituitary it is possible that a hypothalamic lesion sufficient to cause GH deficiency might yield a false positive test due to macimorelin acting downstream from the hypothalamus and directly on the pituitary. Such an effect could be time dependent as a prolonged hypothalamic lesion could eventually lead to the depletion of GH stores in the pituitary. This information about the limitations of the macimorelin diagnostic test should be included in the label.
L1158: ECG monitoring should be recommended for all patients for a minimum of 1 hour and up to 2 hours post dose to look for QT prolongation. Should QT prolongation be observed subjects should continue to be followed until it has resolved.

L1169: a Patient 0903: no hormonal laboratory tests in source documents at study site to support the 3 pituitary hormone deficiencies. PHD at baseline was based on medical history: 35-year old male at study entry in October 2007, born in 1972 with congenital panhypopituitary deficiency due to underdeveloped pituitary gland and hydrocephalus with multiple shunt insertions/replacements from September 1972 up to January 2003. On levothyroxine since 1972, hydrocortisone since 1972 and testosterone since 1989. L-ARG+GHRH stimulation test performed 26Nov2007 (post-randomization) = 0.40 (cutpoint = 4.1 µg/L).
L1170: b Patient 0307: tested at study entry in October 2007 for GH deficiency by the arginine stimulation test. The cutpoint of 5 µg/L was used, per the 2003 AACE guidelines in effect at that time and therefore, the patient was considered GH deficient at baseline. Per the protocol in effect in October 2007, only the L-ARG+GHRH test was allowed to assess baseline GH deficiency. This deviation was documented in the CRF in November 2007 and became minor when in January 2011, the protocol was amended to include Arginine as a provocative test with a cutpoint of 0.4 µg/L, per the 2009 AACE guidelines.
L1171: c Patient 0308: no hormonal laboratory tests in source documents at study site to support the 3 pituitary hormone deficiencies. PHD based on medical history: 25-year old male at study entry in December 2007, born in 1982, had transphenoidal surgery for removal of ACTH secreting pituitary adenoma in 1999 while a teenager, with recurrence and stereotactic radiation in 2002. Bilateral adrenalectomy in 2005 for Cushing's syndrome. On levothyroxine since 2001, hydrocortisone since 2005 and testosterone since 2000. L-ARG+GHRH stimulation test performed 26Dec2007 (post-randomization) = 0.49 (cutpoint = 4.1 µg/L).
L1172: d Patient 0401: hormonal laboratory tests in source documents at study site to support 2 pituitary hormone deficiencies (i.e. TSH and GnRH). In 1980, the patient underwent resection of a large pituitary tumor and following the standard practice started receiving prednisone as hormonal replacement for ACTH deficiency.
